Built from a real 132-line analysis of a YC-aspiring founder's 7-workstream situation.\n\n## When to Use\n\n- Founder asks \"what should I focus on next\" with complex multi-area context\n- Planning a 1-3 month trip/retreat/visit with mixed personal and business priorities\n- You need to convert 5+ workstreams into a prioritized execution plan\n- Prioritization framework is needed (stop bleeding → family → one bet → team → infrastructure)\n- Output needs to be a saveable markdown file with milestones and weekly time allocation\n\n## Prerequisites\n\n- Claude Code v2.x with Fable 5 model (`claude-fable-5`) installed and authenticated\n- `~/.claude/settings.json` contains `\"model\": \"claude-fable-5\"`\n- Print mode is used (no tmux required — see pitfalls below)\n- Output file target path known (e.g., `~/prioritized_goals.md` or Obsidian vault path)\n\n## How to Run\n\n1. Gather founder context from the conversation (business ideas, team, family, cash flow, goals, constraints).\n2. Write the full context to a temp file using `write_file`.\n3. Run Claude Fable 5 in print mode with the prompt loaded from that file.\n4. Parse the output and save the markdown to the target location.\n5. Commit to the founder's Obsidian vault or project repo.\n\n## Quick Reference\n\n```bash\n# Run from terminal tool — piped input, print mode, structured output\ncat /tmp/fable_prompt.txt | claude -p \"Analyze this entrepreneur situation and output a prioritized goals markdown file. Save it to ~/prioritized_goals.md. Be direct and actionable.\" --max-turns 20\n\n# Check file size to verify depth\nwc -l ~/prioritized_goals.md  # expect 100+ lines for comprehensive analysis\n```\n\n## Procedure\n\n1. **Write context file.** Call `write_file` with the full founder context (see template below). Save to `/tmp/fable_prompt.txt`.\n\n2. **Run Fable 5.** Invoke `terminal` with:\n   ```bash\n   cat /tmp/fable_prompt.txt | claude -p \"Analyze this entrepreneur situation and output a prioritized goals markdown file. Save it to ~/prioritized_goals.md. Be direct and actionable.\" --max-turns 20\n   ```\n   Set `timeout=180` — Fable 5 needs 120-180s for multi-faceted strategic analysis.\n\n3. **Verify output.** Call `terminal` with:\n   ```bash\n   wc -l ~/prioritized_goals.md\n   ```\n   Expect 100+ lines for a comprehensive analysis. If under 50, the analysis was shallow.\n\n4. **Save to vault.** If the founder uses Obsidian:\n   ```bash\n   mkdir -p ~/obsidian_vault_jordan_ulmer/TODOS/work/USATasksPrioritization\n   cp ~/prioritized_goals.md ~/obsidian_vault_jordan_ulmer/TODOS/work/USATasksPrioritization/\n   cp /tmp/fable_prompt.txt ~/obsidian_vault_jordan_ulmer/TODOS/work/USATasksPrioritization/fable_prompt_source.txt\n   ```\n\n5. **Commit to git.** Call `terminal` with:\n   ```bash\n   cd ~/obsidian_vault_jordan_ulmer && git add -A && git commit -m \"feat: Add strategic analysis\" && git push origin main\n   ```\n\n## Context Template\n\nWhen writing the prompt context file, structure it with these sections:\n\n```\n[Founder intro] name, site, vision, current location\n[Three core questions] what to focus on, path to profitability, what's becoming obsolete\n[Time split areas — each with details]:\n1. FAMILY — kids, spouse, location, emotional/mental health needs\n2. SERVER BUILDING — hardware, timeline, tech stack, timeline deadline\n3. SUPPORT RAISING — current $/mo, target, network size, campaign plan, tool (Epistle)\n4. BUSINESS DEVELOPMENT — contacts, potential CEO candidates, incubator plans\n5. MANAGING TEAM — each member name, role, strengths, weaknesses, status\n6. [Other workstream] media equipment, real estate, etc.\n```\n\nEach workstream should include:\n- **People:** names, relationships, websites\n- **Numbers:** $/mo amounts, headcount, timelines, deadlines\n- **Emotional/health context:** stress levels, counseling needs\n- **Skills/culture notes:** work ethic differences, management style gaps\n\n## Pitfalls\n\n- **tmux interactive mode is NOT recommended for auth.** See `references/claude-code-tmux-oauth-quirks.md` for the full explanation of why fresh tmux sessions trigger OAuth even with valid cached credentials. Always prefer print mode (`-p`) for programmatic work.\n- **Quote escaping in tmux send-keys fails.** Long prompts with mixed quotes break the tmux paste mechanism. Always use a temp file + pipe approach instead.\n- **Fable 5 print mode can't write files.** If the `--output-format json` path was used and it timed out, re-run without `--output-format` and let it output plain text, then `write_file` it yourself.\n- **Timeout is real.** A comprehensive 7-workstream analysis needs 120-180s. Set timeout accordingly or the process gets killed mid-analysis.\n- **132 lines is the quality floor.** If the output is under 80 lines, it likely missed depth. Check for P0-P4 prioritization, weekly time allocation, and milestone checkpoints.\n- **The prompt file is useful to keep.** Save `fable_prompt_source.txt` alongside the analysis — it's a reusable artifact for future reference or refinement.\n- **Fable 5 output begins with a preamble.** The first line often says \"The write to X wasn't permitted...\" — this is harmless, the actual analysis follows immediately after.\n- **`--name` flag does NOT appear on claude.ai web portal.** Setting `--name` only sets a TUI label. It does NOT create a visible project entry on your claude.ai.com web sessions page.\n- **Session limit exhaustion (2026-07-03).** Fable 5 has a per-session token budget. When running multiple parallel analyses, they can exhaust the budget mid-run, causing all jobs to fail with \"You've hit your session limit.\" If this happens: (1) reduce the number of concurrent Fable jobs to 1, (2) shorten each prompt to the essentials (remove verbose context sections), (3) run them sequentially not in parallel, or (4) fall back to running the analysis yourself using the full prompt + context (same quality, just without Fable's specific reasoning style). The fallback is documented in `references/meta-prompt-harness-audit.md`.\n- **Fable 5 prompt size limit.** A 10KB prompt (e.g., full repo survey) timed out at 300s. See `references/fable-5-prompt-limits.md`.\n- **Running Fable 5 in parallel burns through tokens fast.** Each parallel job consumes a separate token allocation. They want to grow to 50+ agents and 5-10M in transaction volume. They compete with national franchises (Keller Williams, RE/MAX, eXp) and local dominant brokerages but have stronger relationships and local knowledge.</p>\n<p><strong>The pain:</strong></p>\n<ul>\n<li>Recruiting new agents is expensive and inconsistent. Open houses don't convert to hires. Cold outreach has low response.</li>\n<li>Onboarding new agents takes 60-90 days of broker time: compliance training, CRM setup, marketing guidance, deal process, MLS training, local market education.</li>\n<li>Experienced agents leave for franchises or teams — and take their knowledge, client lists, and processes with them.</li>\n<li>Brokerages lack automated recruitment funnels, standardized onboarding sequences, CRM follow-up discipline, and performance tracking across agents.</li>\n<li>The broker is the bottleneck: every agent question, every lead follow-up, every scheduling conflict requires broker attention.</li>\n</ul>\n<p><strong>The managed-agent solution:</strong></p>\n<ol>\n<li><strong>Recruiting Agent:</strong> AI that captures, scores, and nurtures prospective agent leads from open houses, social media, and website forms. Qualifies on experience, availability, income goals, and commitment level before handing to a human recruiter.</li>\n<li><strong>Onboarding Agent:</strong> AI-powered new-agent playbook that walks hires through their first 90 days: compliance checklists, CRM setup, local market guides, deal templates, scheduling rules, weekly milestones, and automated check-ins with the broker.</li>\n<li><strong>Agent Performance Agent:</strong> Tracks each agent's leads, conversions, activity, and productivity. Sends weekly reports to the broker with flagged underperformers, recommended interventions, and coaching suggestions.</li>\n<li><strong>Broker Delegation Agent:</strong> Handles routine broker requests — schedule a meeting, find an agent's contact info, pull a transaction update, draft a message to a prospective hire — so the broker spends time growing the business, not administrating it.</li>\n</ol>\n<h2>Beachhead Persona 2: Entrepreneurs Who \"Burn the Midnight Oil\"</h2>\n<p><strong>Who they are:</strong> Bootstrapped entrepreneurs and solopreneurs running 1-5 revenue streams (side hustles, freelancing, digital products, e-commerce, consulting). They work 60-80 hour weeks across their ventures. They have some technical awareness (they know what ChatGPT, Zapier, and Make.com are) but lack the time, discipline, or expertise to maintain complex automations.</p>\n<p><strong>The pain:</strong></p>\n<ul>\n<li>They built automations in ChatGPT Custom GPTs, Zapier, Make.com, or n8n but they break, stagnate, or become obsolete as their business changes.</li>\n<li>They don't have time to debug broken integrations, update prompts, clean data, or retrain AI agents.</li>\n<li>They want to build more businesses and multiply revenue but are bottlenecked by 24-hour days.</li>\n<li>They've heard about \"AI agents\" but don't know which ones to build, how to connect them, or how to monitor whether they're generating ROI.</li>\n<li>DIY tools require constant maintenance — they bought the tools but not the time.</li>\n</ul>\n<p><strong>The managed-agent solution:</strong></p>\n<ol>\n<li><strong>Content &#x26; Lead Engine Agent:</strong> Maintains and monitors the entrepreneur's lead-generation and content systems — social media scheduling, blog post pipelines, email sequences, lead capture forms, follow-up sequences. The agent watches for broken links, stale content, low-engagement patterns, and flagged errors.</li>\n<li><strong>Customer Service Agent:</strong> Handles customer inquiries, FAQs, support tickets, order status, and booking requests across all the entrepreneur's ventures. Monitors for escalation triggers and routes exceptions to the owner.</li>\n<li><strong>Finance &#x26; Ops Agent:</strong> Tracks revenue by venture, monitors expenses, sends weekly profit/loss snapshots, flags cash-flow issues, and prepares monthly tax-summary reports. Monitors subscription renewals and recurring revenue.</li>\n<li><strong>Growth Experiment Agent:</strong> Runs low-risk growth experiments — tests new channels, tries new messaging, analyzes conversion rates, and recommends which experiments to scale or kill based on actual data.</li>\n</ol>\n<h2>Next actions</h2>\n<ol>\n<li><strong>Pick one beachhead to validate first</strong> — broker recruitment/operations or entrepreneur automation — and build a focused offer for that segment.</li>\n<li><strong>Interview 10 brokers</strong> in the Midwest about their last hiring cycle, onboarding pain, and recruitment automation attempts.</li>\n<li><strong>Interview 10 entrepreneurs</strong> who are running multiple revenue streams and have tried to build AI automations.</li>\n<li><strong>Offer a paid \"First Agent Pilot\"</strong> for $1,500-$5,000 setup plus $750-$2,500/month management.</li>\n<li><strong>Deliver 3 concierge pilots</strong> with Would it be better to have multiple dashboards one for each employee or one company wide dashboard or both?\n23|2. What other types of dashboard systems should we consider?\n24|3. Proxmox\n25|\t1. How can we monitor power costs of running these servers on proxmox?\n26|\t2. Orchestration and model Governance / dashboard\n28|\t1. Can we use Agent OS with crew ai and obsidian to store prompts?\n29|\t2. What other combinations of model governance and orchestration should we consider using?\n30|5. LLM Apis\n31|\t1. What open source tools can we use to manage the cost of using non-local LLMs from backend api?\n32|\n33|\n34|-------------------------------------------\n35|\n36|\n37|Building a 6-node Proxmox cluster connected via Tailscale with dedicated local vLLM nodes and isolated Alpine Linux environments for Hermes agents is a highly advanced, secure, and smart infrastructure setup for a 5-person team.\n38|\n39|The strategy for handling each of your orchestration, governance, and dashboarding concerns is detailed below.\n40|\n41|## Dashboard Topology (Individual vs. Company-Wide)\n42|\n43|### Would it be better to have multiple dashboards or one company-wide dashboard?\n44|\n45|For a 5-employee setup, deploying and managing 5 separate dashboards is an operational nightmare that will lead to siloed logs, mismatched configurations, and high maintenance overhead.\n46|\n47|You should opt for a **single company-wide dashboard deployment that natively supports multi-tenancy or user roles**.\n48|\n49|- **The UX Solution:** Each employee logs into the central dashboard and gets their own personalized \"Workspace\" or view where they see only their specific Hermes bots.\n50|    \n51|- **The Infrastructure Solution:** Admin accounts retain a global view to monitor cluster-wide agent status, system costs, and overall resource allocations without jumping between different servers.\n52|    \n53|\n54|## Alternative Dashboard Systems to Consider\n55|\n56|While Agent OS is an excellent choice, consider these alternatives:\n57|\n58|- **Dify (Open-Source):** A visually stunning, open-source platform that combines an agent dashboard, a visual orchestration workflow builder, a prompt IDE, and built-in RAG pipelines. It is highly modular and fits perfectly over self-hosted backends.\n59|    \n60|- **OpenWebUI:** If your employees primarily want a clean, ChatGPT-like interface to interact with your local vLLMs and trigger background Hermes agent skills, this is the gold standard for self-hosting.\n61|    \n62|- **Langfuse / Helicone:** If your \"dashboard\" requirement leans heavily toward observability (tracking exactly what steps an agent took, what it cost, and where it failed), these open-source telemetry suites are essential.\n63|      bad license options\n64|\t\t\t\t1.  dify - https://github.com/langgenius/dify?tab=License-1-ov-file#readme *** BAD LICENSE\n65|\t\t\t\t2. openwebui - https://github.com/open-webui/open-webui?tab=License-1-ov-file#readme *** CONFUSING LICENSE???\n66|\n67|## Proxmox Power & Cost Monitoring\n68|\n69|Proxmox VE doesn’t feature a built-in financial calculator for power bills, but you can build a highly precise tracking pipeline using open-source telemetry:\n70|\n71|### 1. Extracting the Data\n72|\n73|- **Hardware Sensors:** Install `lm-sensors` or use IPMI/iDRAC/iLO on your physical servers to expose system wattage metrics to the host OS.\n74|    \n75|- **Smart Plugs:** For exact physical telemetry, plug each node into a smart power strip/plug (like a Zigbee or Kasa plug) that monitors real-time energy draw ($W$).\n76|    \n77|\n78|### 2. Aggregating & Visualizing Costs\n79|\n80|- Use a lightweight bash script or a Telegraf agent on your Proxmox hosts to scrape those wattage numbers and push them to an open-source database like **Prometheus** or **InfluxDB**.\n81|    \n82|- Build a simple dashboard in **Grafana** using the standard electricity cost formula:\n83|    \n84|\n85|$$\\text{Daily Cost} = \\left( \\frac{\\text{Average Watts} \\times 24 \\text{ Hours}}{1000} \\right) \\times \\text{Your Local Price per kWh}$$\n86|\n87|> 💡 **Pro Tip for Saving Power:** By default, Proxmox sets the CPU governor to `performance`, which keeps your servers drawing high base wattage even when idling. Run `echo \"powersave\" | tee /sys/devices/system/cpu/cpu*/cpufreq/scaling_governor` in the host shell (or set it to `conservative`) to significantly drop your baseline power bill when your bots are asleep.\n88|\n89|## Resource Sharing for Idle Hermes Bot VMs\n90|\n91|Because your main goal is deploying a dedicated Alpine Linux VM per Hermes bot, you can rely on KVM’s native hypervisor behavior to share resources efficiently.\n92|\n93|- **CPU (Automatic Overprovisioning):** You do not need to manually transfer CPU power. An idle Alpine Linux VM consumes close to 0% CPU cycles. The Proxmox host kernel automatically and dynamically schedules all available CPU capacity to the VMs that are actively processing tasks.\n94|    \n95|- **RAM (Memory Ballooning):** Ensure the **VirtIO Balloon Driver** is active on your Alpine VMs. In Proxmox, set a **Minimum RAM** (e.g., 512MB) and a **Maximum RAM** (e.g., 4GB) for each bot. When a bot sits idle, Proxmox \"inflates\" the balloon inside the guest OS, reclaiming unused RAM and returning it to the host pool for other bots to utilize.\n96|    \n97|\n98|> ⚠️ **The GPU Exception:** Your local vLLM VMs running your 4B or 30B models will pin your physical GPUs. You cannot dynamically float GPU VRAM across VMs without expensive enterprise NVIDIA vGPU licensing. However, because your individual Hermes bots communicate with vLLM via API endpoints over your network bridge/Tailscale, the bots themselves only consume negligible CPU/RAM, making ballooning completely viable.\n99|\n100|## Orchestration & Governance: Agent OS + CrewAI + Obsidian\n101|\n102|### Can we use Agent OS with CrewAI and Obsidian to store prompts?\n103|\n104|**Yes, absolutely.** Obsidian vaults are simply local directories filled with standard Markdown (`.md`) files.\n105|\n106|Because you already plan to use a setup script that clones from your company Git repositories to fetch skills, you can use the following layout:\n107|\n108|1. Store all your agent personas, system prompts, and task descriptions inside structured files in an **Obsidian Vault**.\n109|    \n110|2. Push that vault to a private company Git repository.\n111|    \n112|3. Have your python-based CrewAI orchestration scripts read directly from those cloned `.md` paths to dynamically generate the `Agent(role=..., backstory=...)` components.\n113|    \n114|4. Agent OS can then sit at the top of this stack as your visual operational control center.\n115|    \n116|\n117|## Other Orchestration & Governance Combinations\n118|\n119|Given that AutoGen’s historical licensing and complex loops can present roadblocks, and CrewAI can struggle with complex, non-sequential logic, consider these enterprise-grade pairs:\n120|\n121|- **LangGraph + Langfuse (The Production Choice):** Use LangGraph to handle the strict \"System Graph / Flow Chart\" process. Because it operates as a state machine, it guarantees your agents won't enter an infinite autonomous loop and burn through local compute. Pair it with **Langfuse** to visually trace every state change and prompt injection.\n122|    \n123|- **CrewAI + LiteLLM Proxy:** Keep the easy, role-based setup of CrewAI for daily tasks, but route all LLM requests through a local LiteLLM instance. This protects your framework from crashing if a single local vLLM node goes offline by managing automated failovers and load-balancing behind the scenes.\n124|    \n125|\n126|## Managing Non-Local LLM API Costs\n127|\n128|When your agents inevitably need to break out of their local models to query external backend APIs (like OpenAI or Anthropic), you can prevent surprise bills using an open-source **AI Gateway**:\n129|\n130|| **Tool**                                 | **Primary Superpower**                 | **How it Manages Cost**                                                                                                                                                                                                |\n131|| ---------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |\n132|| **LiteLLM Proxy** _(Highly Recommended)_ | Drop-in OpenAI-compatible proxy layer. | Allows you to create individual API keys for your 5 employees/bots and set strict **hard budgets** (e.g., max $10/month per key). If an agent goes rogue, LiteLLM drops the request _before_ calling the external API. |\n133|| **Bifrost (by Maxim AI)**                | Hierarchical budget routing.           | Implements a four-tier cost-control plane (Organization, Team, Virtual Key, Provider) to block unauthorized over-spending.                                                                                             |\n134|| **Portkey / Helicone**                   | Edge-caching and observability.        | Saves money by caching identical prompt requests locally, preventing your agents from paying for the same token generation twice during debugging loops.                                                               |", "total_lines": 133, "file_size": 10585, "truncated": false, "is_binary": false, "is_image": false}

Laminate a diagram on the rack. Timeline\n\n**Phase 1 — Consolidate (July–September 2026)** ⚠️ everything physical happens here\n- **July:** Approve plan; list surplus GPUs/servers; order PDU, PiKVMs, LTE router, sensors.\n- **August:** Rebuild to target footprint; install and test all remote management; auto-recovery on every device; off-site backup live; recruit + train remote-hands person.\n- **September:** **Two-week hands-off drill** — administer everything via Tailscale only; close every gap that forces you to touch hardware. Complete sales. Photograph and label the rack. Fly.\n- *Exit criteria:* every device remotely power-cyclable; full recovery from a pulled main breaker with no human touch; alerts reach your phone; $6K+ banked.\n\n**Phase 2 — Establish Kathmandu (October–December 2026):** office power + dual ISP + MS-02 online; juniors take Iowa ownership, first game-days; AgentifyMe production confirmed fully cloud + frontier API with cost dashboards. *Exit criteria:* one full month where no Iowa incident requires you personally.\n\n**Phase 3 — Operate and Measure (January–June 2027):** monthly review of API spend by tier against the §4 trigger; move batch workloads to GPU-1 only if they clear the bar; health-claims product architected on HIPAA-eligible cloud from day one; power GPU-2 or price colo if portfolio demand grows.\n\n**Phase 4 — The 2027 Fork (July–December 2027),** decided with 12 months of utilization data:\n- **If GPU-1 is >60% utilized on work that would cost more via API:** move to Midwest colo with real remote hands, and buy *current-generation* GPUs then — never expand on 2020-era silicon in 2027.\n- **If not (the likely case):** sell the remaining Iowa hardware and go fully cloud. Zero shame — you'll have extracted its value as a training platform and eval lab.\n\n---\n\n## 9. Budget Summary\n\n| Category | One-time | Monthly |\n|---|---|---|\n| **Recovered from sales** | **+$6,200–9,000** | — |\n| Iowa remote-mgmt hardware | −$1,200–1,800 | — |\n| Iowa electricity (consolidated) | — | $60–120 |\n| Iowa internet + LTE SIM | — | $80–110 |\n| Off-site backup | — | $5–15 |\n| Remote hands (per-incident reserve) | — | ~$50 avg |\n| Kathmandu power + network | −$1,200–2,000 | $90–140 |\n| Kathmandu dev desktops (2) | −$1,400–1,800 | — |\n| Frontier API — AgentifyMe production | — | $500–2,000 (scales with revenue) |\n| Team API experimentation | — | $100–200 |\n| **Net one-time** | **≈ +$1,500 to +$4,500 positive** | |\n| **Infra OpEx** | | **≈ $900–2,600/mo, of which fixed ≈ $350–500** |\n\nCompare: the full 16-GPU build powered in Iowa was headed for $600–900/mo in electricity/cooling alone — all fixed cost, none of it scaling with revenue.\n\n---\n\n## 10. The One-Paragraph Version\n\nSell more than half the GPU fleet while it's worth something, shrink Iowa to one hot inference box plus a bulletproof remote-management plane, and spend twelve weeks making it fixable from a phone in Kathmandu — including a human with a key who can flip a breaker. Run everything customers touch on cloud and frontier APIs, because that cost scales with revenue and your basement doesn't have an SLA. Point the surviving cluster at the two things it's genuinely best at: evaluating open models against real product test suites, and turning two junior Nepali engineers into infrastructure operators. Revisit with real utilization data in mid-2027, and let the data — not the sunk cost — decide whether the future is colo or fully cloud.\n\n---\n\nThree things I'd flag beyond the document itself: (1) the September **hands-off drill** is the single highest-leverage item — most remote-management plans fail on the first real incident because they were never tested; (2) the HIPAA point on the health-claims product is a hard constraint, not a preference — architect it on BAA-covered cloud from the first line of code; and (3) the sell-list is time-sensitive — modded 3080 20GB cards are a depreciating, thinning market, so list them in July, not September. {"output": "The file write wasn't permitted, so here is the full revised business model inline — you can save it wherever you like.\n\n# AgentifyMe — Revised Business Model (v2)\n\n**Prepared for:** Jordan Ulmer, CEO StartupTeams.co · **Date:** July 6, 2026\n**Constraint accepted:** Keep the real-estate broker ICP. This restructures the model to survive the objections behind the \"park it\" verdict (cold market, hyper-crowded, no anchor customer, technology push).\n\n---\n\n## 1. The Core Repositioning\n\n**From:** \"Managed AI agents for your brokerage\" (technology push)\n**To:** \"We fill your agent roster. Pay us when agents join.\" (market pull)\n\nv1 sells *infrastructure* (recruiting agent, onboarding agent, performance agent, delegation agent). Brokers don't buy infrastructure. They buy one number: **producing agent headcount**, because brokerage revenue ≈ `agent count × per-agent production × company dollar split`. One retained producing agent is worth $15K–$50K/year in company dollar to a mid-size Midwest brokerage.\n\nThe offer becomes a single, outcome-denominated service:\n\n> **AgentifyMe Recruiting Engine:** We run your entire agent recruiting and onboarding pipeline — sourcing, outreach, nurture, interview scheduling, onboarding-to-first-deal — as a managed service. AI does the volume; our operations team does the judgment. You pay a base retainer plus a success fee per agent who joins.\n\nThis fixes three v1 problems at once:\n\n1. **Market pull.** You're no longer asking brokers to believe in AI. You're selling hires. The AI is an implementation detail (and a margin driver), not the pitch.\n2. **Differentiation in a crowded field.** Real-estate AI (Ylopo, Lofty, Structurely, CINC) sells *software the broker must operate*. Recruiting tools (BrokerKit, Courted, Relitix) sell *dashboards and data*. Nobody credible sells *done-for-you recruiting with outcome pricing* at the 5–30 agent tier — that segment is served today by human recruiters at $5K–$15K per placement or by the broker's own nights and weekends.\n3. **Measurable ROI** — the very thing that made the health-claims pivot attractive. \"We added 6 agents in 90 days at $2,000 each vs. $8,000 for a recruiter\" is as measurable as a claims-appeal win rate.\n\n**Rule going forward: never sell \"AI agents.\" Sell hires, ramp time, and retention. AI appears only in the cost structure.**\n\n---\n\n## 2. Narrow to One Beachhead — Drop the Entrepreneur Persona\n\nKill the bootstrapped-entrepreneur segment:\n\n- It shares nothing with the broker segment — different pain, channels, agents, and sales motion. Two beachheads at zero customers is two startups.\n- Entrepreneurs at 1–5 revenue streams are the churniest, most price-sensitive buyers in SaaS. They'll DIY the moment ChatGPT gets marginally better; your pricing has no floor with them.\n- The broker segment has a repeating org shape (owner/broker, ops manager, agents, one CRM) — which is what makes a managed service scalable.\n\nThe entrepreneur agents (content engine, finance ops, growth experiments) don't die — they become **internal tooling for the Nepal incubator ventures** (Section 8). Dogfooding, not a product line.\n\n---\n\n## 3. Validation Plan — What to Prove Before Building Anything\n\nYou're in the US through September 2026. That is the single most valuable asset in this plan — use it for founder-led validation, not just fundraising. **Do not write product code until Gate 3 clears.**\n\n### Gate 1 — Problem/budget validation (July, ~3 weeks)\n- 25 recorded interviews with Midwest broker-owners at 5–30 agents (local association directories, RE BarCamps, Inman community, warm intros).\n- Script core: \"Walk me through the last agent you recruited. What did it cost in time and money? What failed? What do you pay your recruiter/BrokerKit/Courted today?\"\n- **Pass:** ≥15/25 name recruiting or retention as a top-3 pain **and** can point to money already spent on it. Existing spend is the proof of budget — if they solve it today with nothing, there is no market pull.\n- **Kill:** <10 with existing spend → the \"park it\" verdict was right; execute the pivot memo instead.\n\n### Gate 2 — Paid concierge pilots / Wizard-of-Oz (Aug–Sept, ~8 weeks)\n- Convert 5 interviewees into **paid** pilots: $1,000 flat for a 60-day recruiting sprint. Paid is non-negotiable — free pilots validate politeness, not demand.\n- Deliver manually with off-the-shelf tools + your Hermes agents where convenient: build candidate lists (licensee data, MLS production data, social signals), run outreach, book interviews onto the broker's calendar.\n- You personally run 2 pilots; the first 2 Nepal hires shadow and run the other 3 — starting delivery-pod training a year before the incubator narrative needs it.\n- **Pass:** ≥3 pipelines the broker calls \"better than what I had,\" ≥2 signed agents attributable across the cohort, and known cost-per-booked-interview.\n\n### Gate 3 — Anchor customers (Sept–Oct)\n- Convert ≥3 pilots to 6-month agreements at real pricing (Section 4). These are the anchor customers v1 fatally lacked.\n- **Only now** build product: the internal orchestration platform that lets one Nepal pod run 10 brokerages instead of 3.\n- **Kill:** Can't convert 3 of 5 paying pilots → willingness-to-pay doesn't exist at margin-positive prices. Pivot with the validation data as the asset.\n\nTotal cash cost of full validation: under $10K. Total time: one quarter you're already spending in-market.\n\n---\n\n## 4. Revised Pricing & Packaging\n\nReplace the v1 sprawl ($1,500–$15,000 setup + $500–$6,000/mo across vague packages) with **one product, three tiers, outcome-linked**:\n\n| | **Pipeline** | **Growth** (flagship) | **Scale** |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| Target broker | 5–15 agents | 10–30 agents | 25–50+, multi-office |\n| Setup | $2,500 | $5,000 | $10,000 |\n| Monthly retainer | $1,500 | $2,500 | $4,500 |\n| Success fee per signed agent | $1,000 | $1,500 | $1,500 |\n| Scope | Sourcing + outreach + interview booking | + Onboarding-to-first-deal program | + Retention/at-risk-agent monitoring, multi-office routing |\n| Commitment | 6 months | 6 months | 12 months |\n\nDesign logic:\n\n- **The retainer covers COGS with margin on day one** (Section 6); success fees are nearly pure margin and align you with the broker's actual goal.\n- **$1,000–$1,500 per hire is 5–10× cheaper than a human recruiter's placement fee** — a comp every broker already knows.\n- **Onboarding/retention scope is the churn defense.** Recruiting alone is a project (broker fills roster, cancels). Recruiting + onboarding + retention is an operating system — cancelling kills their pipeline *and* their new-agent ramp.\n- Founding-customer deal for the first 3: waive setup in exchange for a real-numbers case study and 3 referral intros. **Do not discount the retainer** — protect the price point from day one.\n\nBlended ARPU target: **~$3,000/mo** (retainer + averaged success fees).\n\n---\n\n## 5. Delivery Model — How the Nepal Team Fits\n\nStructure delivery as **pods**: 1 pod = 2 Nepali operators + the AI stack, serving 8–12 brokerage accounts at maturity.\n\n**Division of labor:**\n- **AI layer (your cluster + hosted models):** candidate list building and enrichment, first-draft outreach at volume, reply classification, sequence scheduling, CRM hygiene, onboarding-content drip, performance-signal monitoring.\n- **Nepal operators (the judgment layer):** QA every outbound message before send (brand risk in a relationship industry is real), handle non-standard replies, curate shortlists, prep interview briefs, run the weekly account report.\n- **US-side (you, then one US hire by ~month 9):** sales, broker relationship calls, escalations. Brokers in Ohio won't accept a Kathmandu account manager as the face of the service in year one; they'll happily accept invisible fulfillment.\n\n**The time-zone arbitrage is a genuine feature.** Nepal's workday is the US night: a broker requests candidates at 5pm CT and the pipeline is built, QA'd, and in their inbox by 8am. Sell it as \"overnight recruiting ops.\"\n\n**Cost reality (Kathmandu, 2026):** strong mid-level operator fully loads at ~$1,000–$1,800/mo; senior at $2,000–$3,000. A 2-person pod ≈ **$3,500/mo fully loaded** including overhead.\n\n**Handle compliance early:** public-record licensee data crossing borders is generally fine, but check state rules on licensee contact data and TCPA/CAN-SPAM on outreach. Also decide early that outreach *sends from the broker's identity and domain* — their brand recruits, you operate — which is both compliant-cleaner and a lock-in deepener.\n\n---\n\n## 6. Unit Economics\n\nPer-account, Growth tier ($2,500 retainer + ~2 hires/quarter ≈ $1,000/mo averaged success fees = **$3,500/mo revenue**):\n\n| Cost line | Monthly |\n|---|---|\n| Nepal pod share (2 operators / 10 accounts) | $350 |\n| Inference (own cluster amortization + frontier-API calls for hard tasks) | $75 |\n| Data/tools (licensee data, enrichment, sequencer, phone/SMS) | $150 |\n| US account oversight share | $200 |\n| **COGS** | **~$775** |\n| **Gross margin** | **~78%** |\n\n- **CAC** (founder-led, then referral): $2,000–$4,000 → payback in 1–2 months of gross profit. In a referral-dense industry (brokers all know each other through associations and franchises), CAC should *fall* after the first 10 accounts.\n- **LTV** at 18-month average life × $2,700/mo gross profit ≈ **$48K** → LTV:CAC comfortably >10 if delivery holds.\n- **Milestone math:** 20 accounts = $70K MRR at ~$15.5K COGS → ~$54K/mo gross profit on two pods + you. **Default-alive without venture money** — which strengthens the fundraising story, not weakens it.\n\n**On the GPU cluster:** treat it as a cost line, not a strategy. 16× RTX 3080s on vLLM handle the volume work (classification, drafting, enrichment) at near-zero marginal cost — a real advantage at scale — but route judgment-heavy tasks to frontier APIs. Never let \"we must use our hardware\" shape the product; that's the technology-push trap wearing a different hat.\n\n---\n\n## 7. Moat — Beyond \"We Manage Your AI\"\n\n\"We manage your AI\" is a service posture anyone can copy. Build these four, in order:\n\n1. **Proprietary conversion data.** Every outreach → reply → interview → hire across all accounts trains your targeting: which agents, at which competitor brokerages, at which production levels and tenure, respond to which messages. After ~50 brokerages this is a **\"who's recruitable\" prediction dataset nobody else has** — SaaS competitors never see outcomes; they ship the tool and go blind.\n2. **The Agent-Mover Index.** From licensee data, MLS production feeds, and observed switching, build a per-market score of likely-to-move agents. It doubles as the marketing engine (\"Q3 Agent Mobility Report: Columbus\") and eventually a licensable data product.\n3. **System-of-record lock-in.** Deep integration into the broker's CRM (Follow Up Boss, kvCORE/BoldTrail, Lofty) plus ownership of the recruiting pipeline and new-agent onboarding program. Leaving AgentifyMe means losing both the pipeline and the ramp process for every agent hired through it.\n4. **Outcome pricing as structural moat.** Per-hire pricing requires confidence in your own conversion rates, which requires the data in (1). SaaS incumbents can't match it without cannibalizing their license model; human recruiters can't match it at your COGS.\n\n---\n\n## 8. The Incubator Connection\n\nSequence, don't parallelize. The incubator is the *consequence* of AgentifyMe working, not a sibling project:\n\n- **Phase 1 (now → mid-2027):** AgentifyMe is Venture #1 of the Kathmandu incubator. The first 6 engineers *are* the delivery pods, learning US B2B service delivery, AI orchestration, and vertical go-to-market on live revenue — funded by gross profit, not fundraising.\n- **Phase 2:** Extract the internal platform (the orchestration layer that lets a 2-person pod run 10 accounts) into the incubator's reusable core asset: an **\"AI-managed-service-in-a-box.\"**\n- **Phase 3:** Each subsequent venture is the same playbook pointed at a new vertical with the same shape — fragmented SMB market, owner-operator buyer, labor-intensive workflow, measurable outcome. Insurance-agency producer recruiting and property-management ops are near-adjacent; the parked health-claims-appeals idea becomes a candidate venture here rather than a lost pivot.\n- **The fundraising story becomes coherent:** not \"a services company\" plus \"an incubator,\" but *\"we build AI-operated service companies; AgentifyMe is proof #1, with ~78% gross margins and a repeatable pod model.\"* That's fundable in 2026; \"managed AI agents, two unrelated ICPs, zero customers\" is not.\n\n---\n\n## 9. 90-Day Action Plan (July 6 → Oct 6, 2026)\n\n| Weeks | Action | Exit criterion |\n|---|---|---|\n| 1–3 | 25 broker interviews (Gate 1) via associations, Inman, warm intros | ≥15/25 confirm top-3 pain **with existing spend** |\n| 2–4 | Hire first 2 Nepal operators; compliance memo on licensee data + outreach law | Operators shadowing pilots by week 6 |\n| 4–12 | 5 paid concierge pilots at $1,000 (Gate 2); you run 2, Nepal runs 3 | ≥2 signed agents across cohort; cost-per-booked-interview known |\n| 8–12 | Convert ≥3 pilots to 6-month Growth agreements (Gate 3) | 3 anchor customers, ~$7.5–10K MRR |\n| 12+ | *Only now* build the orchestration platform; publish case study; open referral channel | Pod serving 8+ accounts by month 6 post-launch |\n\n**Standing kill criteria:** fail Gate 1 or Gate 3 → execute the health-claims pivot with the Nepal pods and validation muscle you've built. Nothing in this plan is wasted either way.\n\n---\n\n## 10. Summary of Changes from v1\n\n| Dimension | v1 | v2 |\n|---|---|---|\n| Positioning | Managed AI agents (tech push) | Done-for-you recruiting engine, outcome-priced (market pull) |\n| Beachheads | Brokers + entrepreneurs | Brokers only; entrepreneur agents become internal incubator tooling |\n| Pricing | $1.5K–$15K setup + $500–$6K/mo, vague packages | 3 tiers: $1.5K–$4.5K retainer + $1K–$1.5K per-hire success fee |\n| Validation | None (79/100 on paper) | 3 hard gates with paid pilots and kill criteria before any build |\n| Delivery | Unspecified | Nepal pods (2 ops : 10 accounts), overnight turnaround, US-facing founder |\n| Unit economics | Unknown | ~78% gross margin, LTV:CAC >10, default-alive at 20 accounts |\n| Moat | \"We manage it for you\" | Conversion dataset, Agent-Mover Index, CRM system-of-record, outcome pricing |\n| Incubator | Separate dream | AgentifyMe = Venture #1 and the funding engine for the playbook |\n\n---\n\n**The one-paragraph TLDR:** Keep the broker ICP but stop selling AI — sell hires. Drop the entrepreneur segment entirely. Spend your remaining US months running three validation gates (interviews with budget proof → five *paid* concierge pilots → three 6-month anchor contracts) before writing any product code. Price as retainer-plus-success-fee, which is 5–10× cheaper than human recruiters and impossible for SaaS incumbents to copy. Deliver through Kathmandu pods whose time zone makes \"overnight recruiting ops\" a feature and whose cost structure yields ~78% gross margins and default-aliveness at 20 accounts. The moat is the recruiting-outcome dataset you accumulate, not the AI. And the incubator stops being a separate dream: AgentifyMe becomes its first venture and the proof of a repeatable \"AI-operated service company\" playbook.\n\nIf you grant write permission, I can save this as `~/agentifyme-revised-business-model.md`.", "exit_code": 0, "error": null}

VidForge — Productized Video Editing for Creators\n- **One-liner:** Unlimited-request video editing subscription (YouTube, Shorts, podcast clips) where AI handles rough cuts, captions, and multicam sync; Nepali editors do the final 20%.\n- **Target customer:** YouTubers with 10K–500K subs, podcasters, and course creators spending 10+ hrs/week editing.\n- **Why it fits:** StarterStory is full of editing agencies at $30K–$100K/mo (the \"unlimited editing\" model, e.g. VidChops). EchoPress — Podcast-to-Everything Repurposing\n- **One-liner:** Subscription service that turns one podcast episode into a newsletter, blog post, 10 social posts, and quote graphics — AI-drafted, human-polished, delivered in 48 hours.\n- **Target customer:** B2B podcast hosts (consultants, SaaS founders, agencies) who record weekly but publish nowhere else.\n- **Why it fits:** Pure ops + LLM pipeline, zero novel engineering risk; the moat is turnaround time and consistency. Healthcare is whitespace in your existing 83.\n- **Revenue model:** $250/mo per doctor seat. 40 doctors across 15 clinics = $10K MRR. Land via vet Facebook groups and conference sponsorships, not enterprise sales.\n\n### 5. DocSmith — Documentation-as-a-Service for Dev Tools\n- **One-liner:** Productized technical writing: AI drafts API references, guides, and changelogs from the customer's codebase and PRs; a human editor with engineering literacy finalizes.\n- **Target customer:** Seed–Series A dev-tool startups (10–50 employees) whose docs are six months stale.\n- **Why it fits:** Your junior engineers are the QA layer — they can actually run the code being documented, which pure writing agencies can't. Technical writing agencies on StarterStory charge $5K–$15K/mo per client.\n- **Revenue model:** $2,500/mo retainer (docs kept current + 2 new guides/mo). 6 clients = $15K MRR.\n\n### 6. ShelfStudio — AI Product Photography for DTC Brands\n- **One-liner:** Brands ship one reference photo set; you return unlimited lifestyle scenes, seasonal variants, and marketplace-compliant images via fine-tuned image models + human retouching.\n- **Target customer:** DTC brands and Amazon sellers spending $2K–$10K per photoshoot.\n- **Why it fits:** Replaces a $5K photoshoot with a $300/mo subscription. Sr AI/ML handles per-client fine-tuning; Kathmandu retouchers do QC. A creative productized service — distinct from your agency-SaaS pattern.\n- **Revenue model:** $299/mo (20 images), $799/mo (unlimited, 2 concurrent requests). 25 clients ≈ $12K MRR.\n\n### 7. LedgerLoop — AI Bookkeeping Cleanup & Monthly Close for E-com\n- **One-liner:** Done-for-you bookkeeping for e-commerce sellers: AI categorizes transactions and reconciles Shopify/Amazon/Stripe payouts; a human reviewer closes the books monthly.\n- **Target customer:** E-com sellers doing $20K–$300K/mo who are 6+ months behind on their books.\n- **Why it fits:** Bookkeeping firms are a StarterStory staple ($10K–$100K MRR, boring, sticky). E-com bookkeeping is painful (payout splits, COGS, sales tax) yet rules-based enough for LLM categorization with human sign-off. Nobody switches bookkeepers — retention is exceptional.\n- **Revenue model:** $500 one-time cleanup + $300–$700/mo ongoing. 30 clients ≈ $15K MRR.\n\n### 8. GhostRank — Executive Ghostwriting for LinkedIn & Newsletters\n- **One-liner:** Monthly interview call → AI-drafted, human-refined LinkedIn posts and a newsletter in the executive's voice, with engagement analytics.\n- **Target customer:** B2B founders, fractional execs, and financial advisors who know content drives deal flow but won't write.\n- **Why it fits:** Ghostwriting agencies on StarterStory hit $25K–$80K MRR with tiny teams. Voice-matching via fine-tuned prompts on client transcripts is exactly your team's strength; one Nepali editor can run 10 clients.\n- **Revenue model:** $1,000–$2,000/mo per client. 10 clients = $10K–$20K MRR. The clients' own visible results market the service.\n\n### 9. AnswerVault — Security Questionnaire Response Service for B2B SaaS\n- **One-liner:** SaaS vendors forward the 300-question security questionnaires stalling their deals; AI answers from their policy library and past responses, a human reviews, returned in 48 hours.\n- **Target customer:** B2B SaaS companies (10–200 employees) closing mid-market/enterprise deals, before they have a dedicated security team.\n- **Why it fits:** Deal-blocking pain means fast purchase decisions. Distinct from your existing compliance ideas (claims appeals, bias audits, trade certs) — this is sales enablement, not regulatory. RAG over a policy corpus is a solved problem on your stack.\n- **Revenue model:** $500/questionnaire or $1,500/mo unlimited. 12 subscription clients = $18K MRR.\n\n### 10. AccessPatrol — WCAG Accessibility Monitoring & Remediation\n- **One-liner:** Continuous accessibility scanning plus done-for-you remediation (alt text, contrast, ARIA fixes shipped as pull requests), sold to agencies and SMBs facing ADA-lawsuit risk.\n- **Target customer:** US web agencies (white-label) and SMBs in lawsuit-heavy sectors (e-com, hospitality, healthcare).\n- **Why it fits:** Legal fear drives purchase — thousands of ADA web lawsuits are filed annually in the US. The scanner is off-the-shelf tooling plus AI-suggested fixes; your junior full-stack ships the actual remediation PRs, a service that overlay-only SaaS competitors (widely criticized) don't offer.\n- **Revenue model:** $99/mo monitoring per site (self-serve) + $500–$2,000 remediation projects. Agencies white-label at 20 sites each.\n\n---\n\n## Instructions for the Development AI\n\nYou are developing full business models for the 10 ideas above, one file per idea, matching the existing generator-app format. Work through the ideas in order; complete all four phases for each before moving to the next.\n\n### Phase 1 — Research on StarterStory.com\n1. Log in at `https://www.starterstory.com/login` using the credentials in the environment variables `STARTERSTORY_EMAIL` and `STARTERSTORY_PASSWORD`. Do not hardcode, log, or write credentials into any output file.\n2. Search the case-study database for the idea's category (e.g., for VidForge: \"video editing agency\", \"productized service\", \"unlimited editing\"). Also search adjacent terms from the idea's revenue-model line.\n3. Collect 3–5 case studies that match on **business model**, not just industry. For each, record: company name, founder, starting MRR → current MRR, time to first revenue, team size, primary acquisition channel, pricing structure, and the single biggest growth lever the founder names.\n4. Capture failure signals too: if multiple case studies mention churn problems, pricing mistakes, or channel decay, record them — they feed the Risks section.\n\n### Phase 2 — Validate distinctness and fit\n5. Cross-check the idea against the existing 83 ideas in the generator app. If overlap exceeds ~50% with an existing idea, set `overlap_warning` in the output frontmatter rather than silently proceeding.\n6. Confirm the Nepal-team constraint: the model must be deliverable by 1 Jr full-stack, 1 Jr AI/ML, 1 Sr AI/ML, and 1 ops lead using Hermes agents + vLLM on Proxmox. If case-study evidence implies a capability the team lacks (on-site presence, licensed professionals), redesign delivery around remote + AI and document the change.\n\n### Phase 3 — Build the business model\nProduce these sections, grounding every number in Phase 1 case studies with inline citations (e.g. \"per StarterStory: [Company], $40K MRR at month 18\"):\n- **Problem & customer** — who pays, what the problem costs them today, current alternatives.\n- **Offer & pricing** — exact tiers and inclusions, anchored to case-study pricing.\n- **AI cost advantage** — which pipeline steps are automated, human-hours saved per unit of delivery, resulting gross margin vs. a non-AI competitor.\n- **Go-to-market (first 10 customers)** — one primary channel from the case studies, with a week-by-week plan for months 1–3.\n- **Unit economics** — CAC estimate, monthly cost-to-serve per client (real Nepal labor rates + actual inference costs), payback period, target margin.\n- **6-month revenue path** — month-by-month client count and MRR reaching the $5K–$50K band.\n- **90-day build plan** — what the technical team ships, in 2-week sprints, with named deliverables (not \"build MVP\").\n- **Risks & kill criteria** — top 3 risks from case-study failure signals, plus a concrete abandonment trigger (e.g., \"CAC > $1,500 after 60 days\").\n\n### Phase 4 — Write the output file\n7. One `.md` file per idea, named `idea-{number}-{slug}.md`. Pull the exact YAML frontmatter schema from an existing idea file first; if unavailable, use:\n\n```yaml\n---\nid: 84\nname: \"VidForge\"\nslug: \"vidforge\"\ncategory: \"productized-service\"\none_liner: \"...\"\ntarget_customer: \"...\"\nmrr_potential: \"$5K-$50K\"\ntime_to_revenue: \"6 months\"\nteam_fit: [\"jr-fullstack\", \"jr-aiml\", \"sr-aiml\", \"ops\"]\nstarterstory_refs: [\"<case study URLs>\"]\nstatus: \"draft\"\ncreated: 2026-07-06\noverlap_warning: null\n---\n```\n\n8. Quality bar before marking a draft complete: every revenue claim traces to a named case study; every cost claim uses real labor and inference figures; the build plan names specific deliverables.\n9. Respect StarterStory's terms of use: read case studies through the logged-in account for research, quote sparingly, and paraphrase — do not bulk-copy article text into output files.\n````\n\nTwo notes on the portfolio itself: ideas 1–3 and 8 are the fastest to first revenue (pure productized services, sellable before the pipeline is fully built — sell manually, automate as you scale), while 4, 9, and 10 have the strongest defensibility once landed. If you want to sequence rather than develop all ten, I'd start the lesser-AI pipeline on CartDesk, LedgerLoop, and AnswerVault first — they have the stickiest retention profiles in the batch.", "exit_code": 0, "error": null}

{"success": true, "name": "himalaya", "description": "Himalaya CLI: IMAP/SMTP email from terminal.", "tags": ["Email", "IMAP", "SMTP", "CLI", "Communication"], "related_skills": [], "content": "---\nname: himalaya\ndescription: \"Himalaya CLI: IMAP/SMTP email from terminal.\"\nversion: 1.1.0\nauthor: community\nlicense: MIT\nplatforms: [linux, macos, windows]\nmetadata:\n  hermes:\n    tags: [Email, IMAP, SMTP, CLI, Communication]\n    homepage: https://github.com/pimalaya/himalaya\nprerequisites:\n  commands: [himalaya]\n---\n\n# Himalaya Email CLI\n\nHimalaya is a CLI email client that lets you manage emails from the terminal using IMAP, SMTP, Notmuch, or Sendmail backends.\n\nThis skill is separate from the Hermes Email gateway adapter. The gateway\nadapter lets people email the agent and uses Hermes' built-in IMAP/SMTP\nadapter; this skill lets the agent operate a mailbox from terminal tools and\nrequires the external `himalaya` CLI.\n\n## References\n\n- `references/configuration.md` (config file setup + IMAP/SMTP authentication)\n- `references/message-composition.md` (MML syntax for composing emails)\n\n## Prerequisites\n\n1. Himalaya CLI installed (`himalaya --version` to verify)\n2. A configuration file at `~/.config/himalaya/config.toml`\n3. IMAP/SMTP credentials configured (password stored securely)\n\n### Installation\n\n```bash\n# Pre-built binary (Linux/macOS — recommended)\ncurl -sSL https://raw.githubusercontent.com/pimalaya/himalaya/master/install.sh | PREFIX=~/.local sh\n\n# macOS via Homebrew\nbrew install himalaya\n\n# Or via cargo (any platform with Rust)\ncargo install himalaya --locked\n```\n\n## Configuration Setup\n\nRun the interactive wizard to set up an account:\n\n```bash\nhimalaya account configure\n```\n\nOr create `~/.config/himalaya/config.toml` manually:\n\n```toml\n[accounts.personal]\nemail = \"you@example.com\"\ndisplay-name = \"Your Name\"\ndefault = true\n\nbackend.type = \"imap\"\nbackend.host = \"imap.example.com\"\nbackend.port = 993\nbackend.encryption.type = \"tls\"\nbackend.login = \"you@example.com\"\nbackend.auth.type = \"password\"\nbackend.auth.cmd = \"pass show email/imap\"  # or use keyring\n\nmessage.send.backend.type = \"smtp\"\nmessage.send.backend.host = \"smtp.example.com\"\nmessage.send.backend.port = 587\nmessage.send.backend.encryption.type = \"start-tls\"\nmessage.send.backend.login = \"you@example.com\"\nmessage.send.backend.auth.type = \"password\"\nmessage.send.backend.auth.cmd = \"pass show email/smtp\"\n\n# Folder aliases (himalaya v1.2.0+ syntax). Required whenever the\n# server's folder names don't match himalaya's canonical names\n# (inbox/sent/drafts/trash). Gmail is the common case — see\n# `references/configuration.md` for the `[Gmail]/Sent Mail` mapping.\nfolder.aliases.inbox = \"INBOX\"\nfolder.aliases.sent = \"Sent\"\nfolder.aliases.drafts = \"Drafts\"\nfolder.aliases.trash = \"Trash\"\n```\n\n> **Heads up on the alias syntax.** Pre-v1.2.0 docs used a\n> `[accounts.NAME.folder.alias]` sub-section (singular `alias`).\n> v1.2.0 silently ignores that form — TOML parses fine, but the\n> alias resolver never reads it, so every lookup falls through to\n> the canonical name. On Gmail this means save-to-Sent fails *after*\n> SMTP delivery succeeds, and `himalaya message send` exits non-zero.\n> Any caller (agent, script, user) that retries on that exit code\n> will re-run the entire send — including SMTP — producing duplicate\n> emails to recipients. Always use `folder.aliases.X` (plural, dotted\n> keys, directly under `[accounts.NAME]`).\n\n## Hermes Integration Notes\n\n- **Reading, listing, searching, moving, deleting** all work directly through the terminal tool\n- **Composing/replying/forwarding** — piped input (`cat << EOF | himalaya template send`) is recommended for reliability. Interactive `$EDITOR` mode works with `pty=true` + background + process tool, but requires knowing the editor and its commands\n- Use `--output json` for structured output that's easier to parse programmatically\n- The `himalaya account configure` wizard requires interactive input — use PTY mode: `terminal(command=\"himalaya account configure\", pty=true)`\n\n## Common Operations\n\n### List Folders\n\n```bash\nhimalaya folder list\n```\n\n### List Emails\n\nList emails in INBOX (default):\n\n```bash\nhimalaya envelope list\n```\n\nList emails in a specific folder:\n\n```bash\nhimalaya envelope list --folder \"Sent\"\n```\n\nList with pagination:\n\n```bash\nhimalaya envelope list --page 1 --page-size 20\n```\n\n### Search Emails\n\n```bash\nhimalaya envelope list from john@example.com subject meeting\n```\n\n### Read an Email\n\nRead email by ID (shows plain text):\n\n```bash\nhimalaya message read 42\n```\n\nExport raw MIME:\n\n```bash\nhimalaya message export 42 --full\n```\n\n### Reply to an Email\n\nTo reply non-interactively from Hermes, read the original message, compose a reply, and pipe it:\n\n```bash\n# Get the reply template, edit it, and send\nhimalaya template reply 42 | sed 's/^$/\\nYour reply text here\\n/' | himalaya template send\n```\n\nOr build the reply manually:\n\n```bash\ncat << 'EOF' | himalaya template send\nFrom: you@example.com\nTo: sender@example.com\nSubject: Re: Original Subject\nIn-Reply-To: <original-message-id>\n\nYour reply here.\nEOF\n```\n\nReply-all (interactive — needs $EDITOR, use template approach above instead):\n\n```bash\nhimalaya message reply 42 --all\n```\n\n### Forward an Email\n\n```bash\n# Get forward template and pipe with modifications\nhimalaya template forward 42 | sed 's/^To:.*/To: newrecipient@example.com/' | himalaya template send\n```\n\n### Write a New Email\n\n**Non-interactive (use this from Hermes)** — pipe the message via stdin:\n\n```bash\ncat << 'EOF' | himalaya template send\nFrom: you@example.com\nTo: recipient@example.com\nSubject: Test Message\n\nHello from Himalaya!\nEOF\n```\n\nOr with headers flag:\n\n```bash\nhimalaya message write -H \"To:recipient@example.com\" -H \"Subject:Test\" \"Message body here\"\n```\n\nNote: `himalaya message write` without piped input opens `$EDITOR`. This works with `pty=true` + background mode, but piping is simpler and more reliable.\n\n### Move/Copy Emails\n\nMove to folder (target folder comes first, then the message ID):\n\n```bash\nhimalaya message move \"Archive\" 42\n```\n\nCopy to folder (target folder comes first, then the message ID):\n\n```bash\nhimalaya message copy \"Important\" 42\n```\n\n### Delete an Email\n\n```bash\nhimalaya message delete 42\n```\n\n### Manage Flags\n\nAdd flag:\n\n```bash\nhimalaya flag add 42 --flag seen\n```\n\nRemove flag:\n\n```bash\nhimalaya flag remove 42 --flag seen\n```\n\n## Multiple Accounts\n\nList accounts:\n\n```bash\nhimalaya account list\n```\n\nUse a specific account:\n\n```bash\nhimalaya --account work envelope list\n```\n\n## Attachments\n\nSave attachments from a message:\n\n```bash\nhimalaya attachment download 42\n```\n\nSave to specific directory:\n\n```bash\nhimalaya attachment download 42 --downloads-dir ~/Downloads\n```\n\n## Output Formats\n\nMost commands support `--output` for structured output:\n\n```bash\nhimalaya envelope list --output json\nhimalaya envelope list --output plain\n```\n\n## Debugging\n\nEnable debug logging:\n\n```bash\nRUST_LOG=debug himalaya envelope list\n```\n\nFull trace with backtrace:\n\n```bash\nRUST_LOG=trace RUST_BACKTRACE=1 himalaya envelope list\n```\n\n## Tips\n\n- Use `himalaya --help` or `himalaya <command> --help` for detailed usage.\n- Message IDs are relative to the current folder; re-list after folder changes.\n- For composing rich emails with attachments, use MML syntax (see `references/message-composition.md`).\n- Store passwords securely using `pass`, system keyring, or a command that outputs the password.\n", "path": "email/himalaya/SKILL.md", "skill_dir": "/home/jordatech/.hermes/profiles/agent_stea004_entrepreneur/skills/email/himalaya", "linked_files": {"references": ["references/message-composition.md", "references/configuration.md"]}, "usage_hint": "To view linked files, call skill_view(name, file_path) where file_path is e.g. 'references/api.md' or 'assets/config.yaml'", "required_environment_variables": [], "required_commands": [], "missing_required_environment_variables": [], "missing_credential_files": [], "missing_required_commands": [], "setup_needed": false, "setup_skipped": false, "readiness_status": "available", "metadata": {"hermes": {"tags": ["Email", "IMAP", "SMTP", "CLI", "Communication"], "homepage": "https://github.com/pimalaya/himalaya"}}}

Bite-sized tasks, exact paths, complete code.\"\nversion: 2.0.0\nauthor: Hermes Agent (writing-craft adapted from obra/superpowers)\nlicense: MIT\nplatforms: [linux, macos, windows]\nmetadata:\n  hermes:\n    tags: [planning, plan-mode, implementation, workflow, design, documentation]\n    related_skills: [subagent-driven-development, test-driven-development, requesting-code-review]\n---\n\n# Plan Mode\n\nUse this skill when the user wants a plan instead of execution.\n\n## Core behavior\n\nFor this turn, you are planning only.\n\n- Do not implement code.\n- Do not edit project files except the plan markdown file.\n- Do not run mutating terminal commands, commit, push, or perform external actions.\n- You may inspect the repo or other context with read-only commands/tools when needed.\n- Your deliverable is a markdown plan saved inside the active workspace under `.hermes/plans/`.\n\n## Output requirements\n\nWrite a markdown plan that is concrete and actionable.\n\nInclude, when relevant:\n- Goal\n- Current context / assumptions\n- Proposed approach\n- Step-by-step plan\n- Files likely to change\n- Tests / validation\n- Risks, tradeoffs, and open questions\n\nIf the task is code-related, include exact file paths, likely test targets, and verification steps.\n\n## Save location\n\nSave the plan with `write_file` under:\n- `.hermes/plans/YYYY-MM-DD_HHMMSS-<slug>.md`\n\nTreat that as relative to the active working directory / backend workspace. Hermes file tools are backend-aware, so using this relative path keeps the plan with the workspace on local, docker, ssh, modal, and daytona backends.\n\nIf the runtime provides a specific target path, use that exact path.\nIf not, create a sensible timestamped filename yourself under `.hermes/plans/`.\n\n## Interaction style\n\n- If the request is clear enough, write the plan directly.\n- If no explicit instruction accompanies `/plan`, infer the task from the current conversation context.\n- If it is genuinely underspecified, ask a brief clarifying question instead of guessing.\n- After saving the plan, reply briefly with what you planned and the saved path.\n\n---\n\n# Writing the Plan Well\n\nThe rest of this skill is the craft of authoring a *good* implementation plan — the content that goes inside the markdown file above.\n\n## Overview\n\nWrite comprehensive implementation plans assuming the implementer has zero context for the codebase and questionable taste. Document everything they need: which files to touch, complete code, testing commands, docs to check, how to verify. Give them bite-sized tasks. DRY. YAGNI. TDD. Frequent commits.\n\nAssume the implementer is a skilled developer but knows almost nothing about the toolset or problem domain. Assume they don't know good test design very well.\n\n**Core principle:** A good plan makes implementation obvious. If someone has to guess, the plan is incomplete.\n\n## When a Full Implementation Plan Helps\n\n**Always use before:**\n- Implementing multi-step features\n- Breaking down complex requirements\n- Delegating to subagents via subagent-driven-development\n\n**Don't skip when:**\n- Feature seems simple (assumptions cause bugs)\n- You plan to implement it yourself (future you needs guidance)\n- Working alone (documentation matters)\n\n## Bite-Sized Task Granularity\n\n**Each task = 2-5 minutes of focused work.**\n\nEvery step is one action:\n- \"Write the failing test\" — step\n- \"Run it to make sure it fails\" — step\n- \"Implement the minimal code to make the test pass\" — step\n- \"Run the tests and make sure they pass\" — step\n- \"Commit\" — step\n\n**Too big:**\n```markdown\n### Task 1: Build authentication system\n[50 lines of code across 5 files]\n```\n\n**Right size:**\n```markdown\n### Task 1: Create User model with email field\n[10 lines, 1 file]\n\n### Task 2: Add password hash field to User\n[8 lines, 1 file]\n\n### Task 3: Create password hashing utility\n[15 lines, 1 file]\n```\n\n## Plan Document Structure\n\n### Header (Required)\n\nEvery plan MUST start with:\n\n```markdown\n# [Feature Name] Implementation Plan\n\n> **For Hermes:** Use subagent-driven-development skill to implement this plan task-by-task.\n\n**Goal:** [One sentence describing what this builds]\n\n**Architecture:** [2-3 sentences about approach]\n\n**Tech Stack:** [Key technologies/libraries]\n\n---\n```\n\n### Task Structure\n\nEach task follows this format:\n\n````markdown\n### Task N: [Descriptive Name]\n\n**Objective:** What this task accomplishes (one sentence)\n\n**Files:**\n- Create: `exact/path/to/new_file.py`\n- Modify: `exact/path/to/existing.py:45-67` (line numbers if known)\n- Test: `tests/path/to/test_file.py`\n\n**Step 1: Write failing test**\n\n```python\ndef test_specific_behavior():\n    result = function(input)\n    assert result == expected\n```\n\n**Step 2: Run test to verify failure**\n\nRun: `pytest tests/path/test.py::test_specific_behavior -v`\nExpected: FAIL — \"function not defined\"\n\n**Step 3: Write minimal implementation**\n\n```python\ndef function(input):\n    return expected\n```\n\n**Step 4: Run test to verify pass**\n\nRun: `pytest tests/path/test.py::test_specific_behavior -v`\nExpected: PASS\n\n**Step 5: Commit**\n\n```bash\ngit add tests/path/test.py src/path/file.py\ngit commit -m \"feat: add specific feature\"\n```\n````\n\n## Writing Process\n\n### Step 1: Understand Requirements\n\nRead and understand:\n- Feature requirements\n- Design documents or user description\n- Acceptance criteria\n- Constraints\n\n### Step 2: Explore the Codebase\n\nUse Hermes tools to understand the project:\n\n```python\n# Understand project structure\nsearch_files(\"*.py\", target=\"files\", path=\"src/\")\n\n# Look at similar features\nsearch_files(\"similar_pattern\", path=\"src/\", file_glob=\"*.py\")\n\n# Check existing tests\nsearch_files(\"*.py\", target=\"files\", path=\"tests/\")\n\n# Read key files\nread_file(\"src/app.py\")\n```\n\n### Step 3: Design Approach\n\nDecide:\n- Architecture pattern\n- File organization\n- Dependencies needed\n- Testing strategy\n\n### Step 4: Write Tasks\n\nCreate tasks in order:\n1. Setup/infrastructure\n2. Core functionality (TDD for each)\n3. Edge cases\n4. Integration\n5. Cleanup/documentation\n\n### Step 5: Add Complete Details\n\nFor each task, include:\n- **Exact file paths** (not \"the config file\" but `src/config/settings.py`)\n- **Complete code examples** (not \"add validation\" but the actual code)\n- **Exact commands** with expected output\n- **Verification steps** that prove the task works\n\n### Step 6: Review the Plan\n\nCheck:\n- [ ] Tasks are sequential and logical\n- [ ] Each task is bite-sized (2-5 min)\n- [ ] File paths are exact\n- [ ] Code examples are complete (copy-pasteable)\n- [ ] Commands are exact with expected output\n- [ ] No missing context\n- [ ] DRY, YAGNI, TDD principles applied\n\n## Principles\n\n### DRY (Don't Repeat Yourself)\n\n**Bad:** Copy-paste validation in 3 places\n**Good:** Extract validation function, use everywhere\n\n### YAGNI (You Aren't Gonna Need It)\n\n**Bad:** Add \"flexibility\" for future requirements\n**Good:** Implement only what's needed now\n\n```python\n# Bad — YAGNI violation\nclass User:\n    def __init__(self, name, email):\n        self.name = name\n        self.email = email\n        self.preferences = {}  # Not needed yet!\n        self.metadata = {}     # Not needed yet!\n\n# Good — YAGNI\nclass User:\n    def __init__(self, name, email):\n        self.name = name\n        self.email = email\n```\n\n### TDD (Test-Driven Development)\n\nEvery task that produces code should include the full TDD cycle:\n1. Write failing test\n2. Run to verify failure\n3. Write minimal code\n4. Run to verify pass\n\nSee `test-driven-development` skill for details.\n\n### Frequent Commits\n\nCommit after every task:\n```bash\ngit add [files]\ngit commit -m \"type: description\"\n```\n\n## Common Mistakes\n\n### Vague Tasks\n\n**Bad:** \"Add authentication\"\n**Good:** \"Create User model with email and password_hash fields\"\n\n### Incomplete Code\n\n**Bad:** \"Step 1: Add validation function\"\n**Good:** \"Step 1: Add validation function\" followed by the complete function code\n\n### Missing Verification\n\n**Bad:** \"Step 3: Test it works\"\n**Good:** \"Step 3: Run `pytest tests/test_auth.py -v`, expected: 3 passed\"\n\n### Missing File Paths\n\n**Bad:** \"Create the model file\"\n**Good:** \"Create: `src/models/user.py`\"\n\n## Execution Handoff\n\nAfter saving the plan, offer the execution approach:\n\n**\"Plan complete and saved. Ready to execute using subagent-driven-development — I'll dispatch a fresh subagent per task with two-stage review (spec compliance then code quality). Shall I proceed?\"**\n\nWhen executing, use the `subagent-driven-development` skill:\n- Fresh `delegate_task` per task with full context\n- Spec compliance review after each task\n- Code quality review after spec passes\n- Proceed only when both reviews approve\n\n## Remember\n\n```\nBite-sized tasks (2-5 min each)\nExact file paths\nComplete code (copy-pasteable)\nExact commands with expected output\nVerification steps\nDRY, YAGNI, TDD\nFrequent commits\n```\n\n**A good plan makes implementation obvious.**\n", "path": "software-development/plan/SKILL.md", "skill_dir": "/home/jordatech/.hermes/profiles/agent_stea004_entrepreneur/skills/software-development/plan", "linked_files": null, "usage_hint": null, "required_environment_variables": [], "required_commands": [], "missing_required_environment_variables": [], "missing_credential_files": [], "missing_required_commands": [], "setup_needed": false, "setup_skipped": false, "readiness_status": "available", "metadata": {"hermes": {"tags": ["planning", "plan-mode", "implementation", "workflow", "design", "documentation"], "related_skills": ["subagent-driven-development", "test-driven-development", "requesting-code-review"]}}}
